Step 3 had already found that defect (`state.rs:57-645`, mutant `facet:266`), so this wrong pass let no failing item pass. `docs/workflow/specification.md` §8 item 5 therefore draws no second sample.
- *Reclassified as `later decision`.* `text:212`: written before D17 and Q137, and the difference was already a finding at `:104` and `:105`. `text:251`, and `text:173`, `:177`, `:197`, `:228` (re-checkers' `agree` on a corrected derivation): each is `EXCLUDED` under Q145. `change:54`: a D14 default is no difference (below).
- *Derivations the reveal corrected, the recorded line right* (`text:169`, `:284`, `rangeset:327`, `:210`): these stay `agree`.
- *Five of the nine overturns are wrong passes over a missing check where the code matches cm6* (`change:178`, `:250`, `:261`, `rangeset:319`, `text:157`). In two kinds drawn ten times, branch lines and mutant lines, step 4 overturned 2 of 10 each. M30 says such a kind is read whole, so the round starts with that reading (M33).

**E58 and E62.** On the classified rows, the runs above 200,000 overturned 3 of 78 lines and the runs below overturned 0 of 42 (Fisher two-sided p 0.55; 0 of 42 has probability 0.19 at the higher rate). At these sizes no difference is resolvable, as the plan said. M29's allowance stays. For ferrum-view: no extra stratum of short runs, because the class correction catches a length effect anyway (M33). E62: the hook and the audit held (0 rows seen early, after two fixes of the audit's piece of evidence). The per-run cost was right in sum (1.03) but wrong per run, because of the mix of kinds. The figures are revised in M33.

**Q145. A hazard a decision excludes but a written test asserts.** *Decision.* `EXCLUDED`. The verdict now covers two cases: no input a caller can write reaches the hazard, or the caller can still write the input and a decision gives Ferrum another result than cm6's (the copy of `state.cm6.229.d1`, D1's panic inside a character). In the second case the evidence also names the check that asserts the decided result, and without that check the line is a `FINDING`. `EXERCISED` is kept for a check that compares cm6's behavior on the hazard's input. *Why.* An `EXERCISED` line tells its reader that cm6's behavior is proven there. A test of Ferrum's decided result proves the decision is applied, not that Ferrum is cm6, so `EXERCISED` would report more than is checked. The reader of an `EXCLUDED` line checks the decision, and now also the test. *What is relabelled.* The six known lines (`change:74`, `text:173`, `:177`, `:197`, `:228`, `:251`), by the orchestrator, with the re-checker's evidence (plan step 5 (a)). There is no sweep of the 133 `EXERCISED` hazard lines whose evidence cites a row of a deviation class or D11 to D13 (counted at 4fa03f8). No item verdict depends on the label: both labels pass, and an item's deviation shows through its row. A tool cannot tell which check asserts cm6's value and which asserts the decided one, so a sweep would be a reading of 133 lines that changes no result. Step 4's sample puts these labels at about 5%, and the verdict says so in what it does not claim. *Lost:* `EXERCISED` with the decision noted (a reader of the label would take it as cm6's behavior); a third verdict (it adds a label no reader acts on differently from `EXCLUDED` with its check).

**Test labels.** An argument cm6 omits that the Rust call writes with cm6's default (D14) is no difference: the scenario is cm6's. So `test_cm6_change_32` is `EQUIVALENT` as recorded. In `cm6_selection.rs` test 02 the `// literal` comments that mark only such defaults are removed by the test-writer, and that test is then `EQUIVALENT` too (`conformance.md` §0, the card's item 2). The class `facet:10` belongs to is corrected by a tool (`conf_check tests`) over all 197 test lines, not by reading them.

**Q144. G8's two entries.** *What I read.* `ChangeDesc.toJSON` returns cm6's own array (`mirrors/cm6/state/src/change.ts:167`). Ferrum's copy is what `state.cm6.229.d1` decided (no caller can change a description) and N9's form requires (a `serde_json::Value`). For `long.replace`, the cm6 side's flat time is a deduction about V8's deferred strings that nobody has verified. In both cases cm6 has not done the work: its caller's next step does it (`JSON.stringify` of the array; the first read of the line's characters). *Decision.* G8 reads such a call on the pair the caller waits for, the call followed by that step, timed on both sides, with the call alone reported beside it (`conformance.md` G8). The round adds the two pairs. If both grow within 0.25 of cm6's exponent, neither item is `NONCONFORMANT` on G8: `to_json`'s cost on the call alone is listed under `state.cm6.229.d1` in the review of explained differences, and `long.replace` passes. If a pair does not, its call is a G8 finding (E65). `state.imp.010` is settled at acceptance from the long-line pair. `state.imp.018` (a JSON text written straight from the description) is a candidate for the delta: it is a function added beside `to_json`, which breaks no caller. *Lost:* `NONCONFORMANT` on the call alone (it would fail Ferrum for a copy a decision requires, while what the caller waits for may be the same); an exemption for both (the long-line deduction is unverified, and the pair is what checks it); changing `to_json`'s return type now (it breaks N9's form and every caller, for a cost no realistic change reaches: 10^6 sections take 70 ms).

**E60 and the items tool.** The 61 disagreements fall into three groups. In 37 the tool is right: findings, `WEAKER` tests, and rows with no `cm6 deviation:` line. In 16 the line was recorded `DECIDED DEVIATION` and the tool derived `CONFORMANT`. In 14 of those the deviation shows on an item whose own line is missing (the round's Q143 lines settle them). The other two (`state.cm6.211`, `.225`) have only rows of class `reproduce` or `form` and are `CONFORMANT`, so their recorded lines were wrong. In 8 the tool is wrong (`state.cm6.087`, `.110`, `.145`, `.146`, `.157`, `.240`, `.230`, `.244`): their findings sit in private helpers, which the tool ties to no item, so a failing item would have passed. The tool is fixed for every item. It ties a helper's line by the mutation selector's call rule, a finding line carries an `items` key where that rule cannot reach it, and an untied finding fails at the verdict commit (plan step 5 (a)). E60 is `partial`.

**Rows decided from step-3 findings that no decision covered.**
- *`state.cm6.126.d1` (`conformance-state.jsonl:314`, the signed zero).* cm6 returns the same effect when the mapped value is `==` to the old one (`transaction.ts:81`). That shortcut exists for identity, and an effect has none in Ferrum. For every value but one the result is the same value: a JavaScript object is `==` only to itself, and `==` primitives of one type are the same primitive, except that `-0 == 0`. Reproducing it would put `T: PartialEq` on every effect type of every crate for a signed zero that a `map` must return on purpose. Class (b), with a patch that lists the fixtures (expected: none). *Lost:* the bound (cost to every effect type, for one value); a downcast to `f64` and `f32` (it would not reach a caller type that models a number). The cost of reversing it later is small: the bound or the downcast can still be added before the facade.
- *`state.cm6.126.d2` (`:312`, an `undefined` value under the default map).* cm6 deletes it on mapping, and its own documentation says a value type should not include `undefined` (`transaction.ts:89-92`). That is computing with `undefined`, D11 (c). Ferrum keeps the value. The round records cm6's fixtures (an `undefined` value, with `null` as the control), and the patch must list exactly the first.
- *`state.cm6.216.d2` (`conformance-text.jsonl:275`).* `count_column` panics with Ferrum's message when a column would pass `isize::MAX`, a value only a tab size near that bound reaches. A column is a count the crates above turn into positions, so it takes D17's bound. `find_column` only compares its sum with `col`, so a saturating sum gives cm6's result for every argument. This is a `form` row with a `cm6 deviation:` line, as `state.cm6.134.d2`.
- *`state.cm6.160.d1`, amended (`conformance-change.jsonl:217`, `:218`, `:249`).* A JSON number is read as JavaScript reads it, the nearest double, and is accepted when it is a non-negative integer of at most `isize::MAX`. The 2^53 bound of `json_length` gave `Err` for integral doubles cm6 stores.
- *D17's messages*, which no text named: three rows of `design.md` §5.
- *Findings that need no new decision* go to the round as code (plan step 5 (e)): `from_json`'s `Err` for a selection past a re-split document (`state.cm6.017.d1`, `Error::SelectionOutsideDocument`); the nested `tr.state()` (`conformance-state.jsonl:401`); `end_construction` (`:430`); the `appendConfig` chain (`:436`, D11 a); `ChangeSet::of` and `src/text.rs:608`, `:650` without D17's check; `line_at`'s `# Panics`. The clock before the epoch (`:408`, a fraction of a millisecond truncated where V8 is deduced to floor it) stays as it is. No test can set the system clock, no system that edits text runs before 1970, and V8's rounding there is unverified, so it is listed in what the verdict does not claim.

**The round.** Step 5 of the plan now orders it: (a) tools, (b) three blind class reads, (c) fixtures from cm6 by a `spec-auditor`, (d) tests, (e) code, (f) the gates again, (g) three verifiers on what changed, (h) a re-check of their closing lines, (i) the verdict. About 2.0 million tokens. *Why this order.* The class reads come first so that what they find joins the one round, rather than starting a second. Fixtures come before written tests, because a value cm6 produces in a run is the proof's own and needs no hand. Mutants and outcomes are closed by the re-run that no longer lists them, not by a reading. *Why a `spec-auditor` for the fixtures, and not the orchestrator as in Q135.* About forty inputs across five chunk interpreters is reading of sources the orchestrator does not hold. The spec-auditor's role owns the generators, and its run is the pilot of its kind (E66). *Lost:* one round per unit (more runs repeating the same base); fixing without the class reads (two kinds with a 20% wrong-pass rate in the sample, whose misses would pass in silence); a verifier for every line again (the gates show the mutants and outcomes by data).

**Confidence.** High on the classification (each overturn re-run by its re-checker, each reclassification by a rule now written), on Q145 and on E60's diagnosis (the eight items traced to their helpers). Medium-high on Q144: the decision is conditional on the pairs, which measure the deduction. Medium on `state.cm6.126.d1`: a judgment of cost against one value. Medium on the round's budget: three of its kinds have no finished run of the same shape. **Reopens.** A class read that overturns fewer than 5 of 57 (E63); a pair of G8 outside cm6's class (E65); a caller of `StateEffect` in the crates above whose `map` can return a signed zero; a run of the round above 1.15 of its estimate (E66).

## 2026-11-27 · Q146 to Q149: the tools of step 5 (a) tried

*Decided by the consultant (Q146 to Q149, raised by the orchestrator while trying the tools of conformance step 5 (a), 2026-11-27). Binding text: `plan.md` "Conformance" step 5 (a), (b), (d), (e), (g) and the round's cost; `cards/verifier.md` items 2 and 6; `docs/workflow/conformance.md` §0 (`CHANGED`); `raw/decisions.jsonl` `state.cm6.126.d1`; `tools/conf_check.py` `tests`; `tools/oracle_patch.py` (docstring); `tools/README.md`. Method side: `docs/decisions/method.md`, same date, "What a tool that replaces a reading must show". Ledger: E67, E68.*

**Q146. The `// literal` comment of a D14 default.** *Decision.* The comment stays, and the test is `EQUIVALENT`. `conf_check tests` already derives the label that way. Its check that failed on such a comment now only counts them (19). The removal in step 5 (d) is struck. The 23 test lines whose recorded label differs from the derived one are relabelled by script in (a): 13 `CHANGED` become `EQUIVALENT`, and 10 `EQUIVALENT` with a decided `// literal` become `CHANGED`. *Why.* The comment does two jobs, and only one of them was in question. For G3's literal check (`impl_check literals`) it is the stated cause of a literal the Rust call writes and cm6's call does not. That check is the one deterministic guard that no ported test's numbers or strings changed. A tool cannot tell that a `0` is `assoc`'s default, so without the comment the check either fails or has to accept unexplained literals, which would open the very hole it closes. For the label, the comment's cause says whether the scenario changed. A cause that names D14 alone and says the caller writes cm6's default does not change it, and the tool reads exactly that. My decision of the same day ("Test labels": remove the comments) assumed a `// literal` comment exists only to make a test `CHANGED`. That assumption was wrong, and this entry replaces that sentence. *The three D14 comments that name no default* (`*val + 1`, `cm6_facet.rs` test 18 and `cm6_state.rs` tests 08 and 10) stay counted `CHANGED`. If that is an error, it claims less sameness than there is, never more, and no tool could separate "a Rust form of the same expression" from a changed value without reading. *Lost:* removing the comments and teaching `literals` D14's defaults (a table of every default of every signature, for one bit a comment already carries); keeping both checks failing (one of them can never pass).

**Q147. Findings tied to no item.** *Decision.* There are three parts.
- *(1) Code findings the call rule cannot reach* (the `From` impls, a field or a generic bound outside any function, helpers such as `src/charcategory.rs:26`) get an `items` key once, before (b), from a script the orchestrator commits with its printout. The script first joins each item line's cited `src/<file>:<line>` to the finding lines there; for a line the join misses, it uses the items of the behavior ids, row ids and cm6 locations the line's own evidence cites; for a line whose evidence names another line as its root (the 15 `shared` lines of `conformance-facet.jsonl`), it uses that root line's items. The printout says where each key came from. A line none of these sources ties goes to me, and is never guessed.
- *(2) A finding about the proof* (an id `replayer:…`, today `conformance-text.jsonl:162`, `:163` and `conformance-state.jsonl:409`) is tied to no item. While one is open, the verdict's commit fails, whatever the items derive.
- *(3)* The `items` key written in (a) is part of the line the (g) verifier re-judges, so the verifier corrects a key that ties too little.

*Why.* The purpose of `items` is that no failing item passes. A tie can be wrong in two directions, and they cost differently: a tie to too many items shows itself as an item derived worse than it is, which (i) reads in the tool's disagreements, while a tie to too few is silent. So each source of a key is taken in the direction that can only over-tie. A key written once, rather than a join the tool repeats on each run, is used because the join reads the evidence of item lines that (i) replaces with the tool's own output. Repeated, it would read text that no longer exists. A defect of the replayer weakens the evidence of items nobody can list: tying it to every item would make the derivation say nothing, and tying it to none would let it pass. So it blocks the verdict until it is closed, which (d) does. *What the try shows.* On the committed lines the eight items of E60 are met only with the keys, because `state.cm6.087` and `.110` derive `CONFORMANT` without them. (a) is met when `items` prints no untied line other than the `replayer:` ones and the eight derive `NONCONFORMANT` or `UNVERIFIED` (E68). *Lost:* leaving the keys to the (g) verifiers (it would leave the derivation wrong through the round, and closed lines would never be tied); making the join a rule of the tool (above); widening the call rule to trait dispatch (`.into()` reaching `From::from`), which is a larger change for six lines that a key ties exactly.

**Q148. A condition a `# Panics` text omits.** *Decision.* `conf_check panics` keeps what it checks: the message's template, for the outermost public function, both ways. The condition is read. The 26 undrawn panic lines (`PRECONDITION` 18, `UNREACHABLE` 8) join the class reads of (b). Each condition is judged against the `# Panics` of every public function through which its site is reached, and against `raw/throws.jsonl`'s `condition` of the throw the site ports (card item 6). (e) writes the missing conditions. (g) judges again the panic lines of every function whose `# Panics` text the round changed. *Why.* M33 replaced the reading of the 36 panic lines by a tool, on the premise that "whether a `# Panics` states the condition that fires is a fact a run shows". A run shows the message and the function, not which condition of the text the panic met, so the premise holds for half of what the verdict asserts. The other half is the part step 4 found wrong (1 of 10 drawn: the equal-length case of `state.cm6.156.d2`). It is silent in every gate, and it reaches the developer who embeds Ferrum: D13 makes a panic a precondition the caller can test first, and a caller can only test what the text states. By M33's own rule, a kind drawn ten times with one overturn is read whole, since below 20 lines a sample cannot tell a sound class from a weak one. *Why in (b) and again in (g).* The class reads come first so that what they find joins the round's one code run (e). The texts that (e) rewrites (24 functions for messages, plus the conditions) are new text, and only a reading after them judges it. *A tool for conditions, weighed.* A check that every decision row quoting a message is cited by the `# Panics` of its item's functions would catch the one known case. But only one row of 67 quotes a message, so it would be a tool built for one instance, while conditions without a row are the class nobody has measured. *Cost:* about 60,000 tokens in (b) at 2,300 a panic line, and about 50,000 in (g). *Lost:* trusting the message check alone (the omission is the silent kind); one reading at (g) only (an omission found there needs a second code run); a sample (above).

**Q149. The patch of `state.cm6.126.d1`.** *Decision.* The patch writes the value rule and nothing else: `mapped == this.value && Object.is(mapped, this.value) ? this : new StateEffect(this.type, mapped)`. cm6 keeps the old effect wherever the value is the same, and makes a new one, holding the returned zero, only where `==` and `Object.is` disagree. The row's wording is corrected to say this. The 10 fixtures are not on its list, and the list stays empty, as the row of the same day expected. A probe run of `Object.is` alone already changed no committed fixture. This form also keeps cm6's new effect for a `NaN` mapped to `NaN`, so it changes cm6 at exactly the deviation. *Why.* A patch is the reference for the deviation: G5 attributes to the row every new case whose output the patch changes, and fails on any change outside the row's behaviors. The row's wording, "always a new effect", described the mechanism of Ferrum, which has no effect identity. Written into cm6, that wording changes identity records the replayer leaves out (`identity`, op `same`). Those 10 fixtures would then be attributed to a deviation about values, and every fresh case that maps an effect would be attributed the same way. A list padded with fixtures the deviation does not touch is the hidden error G5 exists to prevent. `check` failing on them was the check working, so it stays as it is, and the rule goes where patches are written (`tools/oracle_patch.py`). *Lost:* listing the 10 (they would hide the row's real scope from G5); teaching `check` to ignore records the replayer leaves out (it would accept a patch that writes too much).

**What changes in the later steps.** (a): the relabels of Q146, the keys of Q147, the patch of Q149 committed once `check` passes, and `panics` taken as it stands. (b): the 26 panic lines in C1 (7), C2 (17) and C3 (2), with revised estimates of 152,000, 174,000 and 161,000. (d): the D14 comments stay. (e): the conditions. (f): `tests` and `items` are gates as before, and `items` fails on an open `replayer:` line. (g): the panic lines of changed texts, and the `items` keys. Cost of the round: about 2.1 million.

**Confidence.** High on Q146 and Q149: each follows from what the check it would break is for, and both were tried on the committed data. High on Q147's direction rule, and medium on whether the script's sources tie every line (E68 measures it). Medium-high on Q148: the reading is certain to be needed, and the rate it will find is measured by E67. **Reopens.** A D14-default comment whose value is not cm6's default; a key that tied too little, found at (g) or (i); the panic class read overturning more than 7 of 26 (E67); a fixture that shows a `map` returning the other zero.

**The classification: 8 overturns, all low, and none that passed a failing behavior.**

The seventh, `rangeset:152`, is a fact of the specification stated wrongly in four places. `RangeSet::update` on a non-empty set throws when the ranges that do not fit its layers reach an empty layer out of order, and four texts say it never throws: the decision row `state.cm6.183.d1`, the behavior rows `state.RangeSet.update.B4` and `state.RangeSetUpdate.add.B2`, and the `raw/throws.jsonl` row for `rangeset.ts:527`. The fixtures F0106 and F0107 are right. They are cases whose spill is one range, or stays in order, and the rows generalized from them. Ferrum's code is cm6's, and G5 compares it, so no behavior was at risk. A developer reading the text would be misled, though: a caller relying on "accepted on a non-empty set" panics on a set whose layers happen to differ.

- `state.imp.004` gets cm6's corrected behavior, together with a case of Ferrum's own of the same shape. A change position inside a character is not checked by `EditorState::update`. It panics where the new document is first computed, often in another component's call, while cm6 checks a change's range inside `update`. Moving the check is a change of where a precondition fails. It breaks no signature (Q129), so it is weighed in the delta with the rest of that row. Meanwhile `design.md` §5 says where the check is today, and the texts say so.

*Why.* Q147's rule is that a key ties too many items rather than too few. Each key here is every item whose public call can reach the line, and no more, since these four findings close in the round and their keys then stop mattering. *A gap in the script, left.* A cm6 location inside a top-level function the inventory does not list ties nothing. A rule that tied it through cm6's callers would serve four lines once. *Lost:* tying `:349` only to the facet items its root line had (it would leave `field` and `Transaction.state` untied, and those are the calls that read the store).

**E67, confirmed at its bound.** 7 of 26 were overturned, against 0 to 7, and all 7 share one shape, a reaching function left out. What a reading must keep is the condition under a quoted message. ## 2026-11-27 · Q151: which public functions reach a panic site, by the compiler's call graph

*Decided by the consultant (Q151, raised by the orchestrator after the try of `conf_check panics --reach` at dbc4530, src at bbba3bb). Binding text: `plan.md` "Conformance" step 5 (a) (the bullet "after (b)"), (e) (I3), (f), (g); `design.md` §5 "Which functions document a panic". Method side: `docs/decisions/method.md`, same date, "A call graph of Rust code is the compiler's". Ledger: E72 (refuted), E73.*

**The question.** The tool must give, for each panic site, every public function through which it can fire. Its try reached 3 of the 13 pairs the overturns of (b) name. The two wider call rules the orchestrator measured reached 10 and 13, with 1,770 and 4,255 open pairs no reader named. Q151 also asks three things the plan had not said. Does an `unreachable!` site need text from the functions that reach it? Can a public function rely on a public callee's text with no link? And how is a method call into another file followed?

**What I checked.** `tools/conf_check.py` (`reached`, `fns_with_spans`, `panic_reach`, `site_message`, `impl_types`, `panics`), the orchestrator's `variants.py`, `plan.md` step 5, `design.md` §5, the panic lines of the five `raw/conformance-*.jsonl` and their verdicts, the 7 overturned lines of (b) (all `PRECONDITION`), `panics`' `sites.jsonl`, and the code at the sites I spot-checked (`src/selection.rs:326-347`, `src/rangeset.rs:906-924`, `:1036-1046`, `src/state.rs:351-394`, `src/text.rs:265-280`, `src/facet.rs:1180-1228`). `.scratch/conformance/ferrum-state/panics/reach.jsonl` held 4,310 rows, the output of the widest variant, and not the 311 of the committed rule. I regenerated it with the committed tool.

**The cause.** Every rule measured walks by name. Rust resolves a method call by the receiver's type. A rule without types either drops the call (3 of 13) or ties it to every function of the name (4,255 open pairs: `Transaction::annotation` "reaches" `src/text.rs:479`). The compiler has already resolved every call. The MIR that the pinned toolchain emits for the library (`cargo rustc -p ferrum-state --lib -- --emit=mir`, 5 seconds on the stable 1.97.0, no nightly) names the callee of each call as the types select it (`ChangeDesc::try_map_pos(copy _1, …)`).

**The measurement.** I wrote a prototype on that MIR (`mirreach.py` in my scratchpad, not committed: the committed tool's try re-derives its figures). Its rules are now the plan's. A resolved call is an edge. A body's naming a closure or a crate function used as a value is an edge. A `dyn` or generic `Fn*` call reaches the crate's closures and functions used as values whose argument types match, with a type parameter matching any type. A trait method on a type parameter reaches the crate's impls of it. Code outside the crate is not walked. Run at bbba3bb:
- *The trial:* all 13 pairs of (b) are reached and `open`.
- *Soundness on real data:* of the 73 (site, public function) pairs that recorded runs raised a panic through (`sites.jsonl`), 72 are pairs. The one left, `src/state.rs:201` through `Facet::compute`, is a frame of a closure `compute` builds that calls the test's own function, which calls `field`. That is the caller's call (Decision 2).
- *The two holes the prototype first had,* each found by that check: the call of a diverging function (`-> bbN` in the MIR) and a closure's span (the MIR gives only its head) left out pairs. Then a type match of `dyn` calls without wildcards left out 6 pairs. Each is now a rule.
- *Tightness:* the committed rule ties 152 pairs the graph does not. I read four of them in the code, and none is a call. `EditorSelection::single` builds through the private `new` and never reaches `create`'s checks (`src/selection.rs:326`). `RangeSet::join` builds its layers directly and does not use the builder (`src/rangeset.rs:906`). `EditorState::create` does not reach `EditorSelection::create`, and `Transaction::annotation` does not reach `src/text.rs:479`.
- *Counts:* 428 pairs on 39 sites (the fortieth, `src/rangeset.rs:297`, exists only under `__test`). 134 are at sites whose line is `UNREACHABLE`. At `PRECONDITION` sites, 40 are `stated` and 190 `open`: 64 reached through no other public function, and 126 through one, of which 19 go through a public function that states the site. At `FINDING` sites, 63 are `open`, and (e) changes those sites anyway.

**Decision 1: the call graph is the compiler's.** `--reach` walks the MIR graph by the rules above. It checks itself on every run: every public function and every site maps to a body; every run-observed pair is a pair; and a site in an impl of a trait defined outside the crate fails the tool, because the standard library calls such impls where no MIR call shows it (there are none now). *Why the type match and not the count of arguments.* Rust requires a closure's argument types to equal those of the `Fn` it is called as, so the match drops only impossible pairs. The count alone gives 555 pairs for nothing. *Why every closure and not only those seen to escape.* Detecting escape is a rule that can be wrong, and including every closure costs 2 pairs.

**Decision 2: what is an obligation (the three open points).**
- *A site whose standing line is `UNREACHABLE` needs no text from any function.* `design.md` §5 already says that a function whose inputs cannot meet a condition says nothing. An `UNREACHABLE` line claims that no input from any caller reaches the site. Each of the 11 such lines was read in step 3 and again in step 4 or (b), and none was overturned: all 7 overturns of (b) are `PRECONDITION` lines. That claim can fail in one way: the round changes the site's code or opens a new path to it. So (g) now reads every `UNREACHABLE` line whose holder the round's diff changes, or which a public function reaches at the round's commit and did not reach at dbc4530. Both are facts of the diff and of two runs of the tool.
- *A public function that relies on a public callee's text links it.* Text with no link counts for nothing, because a caller reads the function's own entry. This is `design.md` §5's "names the public function whose text states it", and the 7 overturns were exactly functions relying on a callee in silence.
- *A panic that a caller's own function raises by calling a public function belongs to that call.* `EditorState::create` does not state `field`'s panic when a field's `create` calls `field` for a field the state lacks. The caller wrote that call, and `field`'s text states it. Not walking code outside the crate is what gives this.
- *A pair is discharged by its gateways.* When every path from F to a site passes through public functions whose pairs are listed as unreachable by their inputs, F needs nothing. F can only pass those functions values of their own types, and the listed reason covers every such value. The reason is therefore written over every input the function's types allow, and (g) judges it as that. The discharge is what keeps the reading in proportion: the inner function is judged once, not once per caller.

**Decision 3: the reading has a run of its own, (e) I3.** The 190 + 63 open pairs are a reading: whether a function's own inputs can meet a condition is judgment. No tool settles it, and both kinds of error are silent (a panic left out, or a panic stated that cannot fire). I1 and I2 change code and texts, so the pairs are counted again on I2's commit, and I3 takes them innermost first, with the tool run between batches. The 60 of the plan measured name over-ties and has no meaning on this graph. It is replaced by a measure of the over-ties that remain: if more than 3 in 4 of I3's first batch are listed and not stated, the graph ties more than a reading should carry, and I look at the classes of the reasons for a rule.

**Decision 4: `mutants` and `items` take the union from (f) on (a question nobody asked).** Step 1's mutation selection has the same limit: at bbba3bb the MIR graph reaches 53 private functions from the named functions that the name rule does not reach. Step 1 stated that limit. It was a limit of the tool, not a choice of scope, and a tool now removes it for the cost of background time and of reading the new survivors at (g), which (g) already does. The union keeps every function step 1 selected, so the 18 that the name rule alone reached stay, and the runs stay comparable. `items` takes the union for the same reason. Q147's direction rule says that a tie may be too wide and never too narrow. *Lost:* moving both to the MIR graph alone (it would drop step 1's selections mid-stage); leaving both for `ferrum-view` (thinner evidence where the tool to fix it exists).

**Lost.**
- *The committed rule, and the two wider rules by name.* The trial and their counts.
- *rust-analyzer's index (`rust-analyzer lsif`).* It would resolve method calls too. It is not pinned with the toolchain, and the compiler's MIR is the resolution the built crate uses. I did not try it.
- *The run-based `sites.jsonl` alone.* It sees only the functions a run passes through, and it missed `Transaction::new_doc`. It stays as the check of soundness.
- *Requiring every reaching function to state every site,* with no reading. That writes panics that cannot fire into the text a caller relies on. `design.md` §5 already rejects it.
- *Raising the threshold of 60 to let a rule by name pass.* Thousands of pairs tied by name collisions would be read, and the reads would find nothing.

**What the MIR format costs.** rustc marks `--emit=mir` as output for human readers, with no stable format. The toolchain is pinned, a toolchain change runs every check again (M22), and the tool's self-checks fail on a format they cannot read: a public function with no body, a site with no body, a run-observed pair missed.

**Confidence.** High on the graph: the trial, 72 of 73 run-observed pairs with the one left explained, and four spot checks of name-only ties, none of them a call. High on the three rules of Decision 2, which follow from `design.md` §5's purpose and from where each error would show. Medium on the cost of I3 and on the reliability of the listed reasons until I3's ratio and (g)'s overturns come in (E73). **Reopens.** A run-observed pair the tool misses; a panic omission found after (f) passes; more than 1 in 10 listed reasons overturned at (g); a site in an impl of an outside trait.

## 2026-11-27 · Step 5 (c) after F1: Q152, Q153 and the cut of the rest of the fixtures

*Decided by the consultant (Q152 and Q153 of `ferrum-state`, and the crate's part of M39, whose record is `docs/decisions/method.md`, same date). Binding text: `design.md` §5 (the first paragraph, and a message in the table of Ferrum's own panics) and §10 "Engine errors"; `plan.md` "Conformance" step 5 (c), (d) and (e); `raw/improvements.jsonl` `state.imp.019`. Ledger: E66 (partial result), E74.*

**Q152. A set that lost a text, and the engine's `TypeError`.** *What cm6 does and why.* `invert` does not check that its document has the set's length (`mirrors/cm6/state/src/change.ts:218-232`). Q11 decided to reproduce that (`state.cm6.140.d1`). On a shorter document a replaced range past the end gets an empty text. `map` then drops that text, because `addInsert` returns at once on an empty value (`:393-394`). The mapped set has an insertion section with no entry in `inserted`. cm6 reads `inserted[i]` without a guard in three places: `toJSON` (`:305`), `iterChanges` (`:416`, through `Text.append` of `undefined`, which also reaches `apply`) and `SectionIter.textBit` (`:563`, through `compose`). Each fails with a V8 `TypeError` (F8018 records two of them). The `text` getter (`:557`) does guard: it gives `Text.empty`, and that is how `map` dropped the text. None of this is a contract of cm6. It is JavaScript failing on an array that ran short. For `:416` I deduced the failure from `Text.replace` reading `text.length`; I did not run it. F2's throws rows run it.

*Decision.* D13 already settles the class. Where cm6 fails with an engine `TypeError` on input Ferrum's types can express, Ferrum panics at the same condition with a message of its own. `design.md` §5 now lists that message: `The change set holds no text for one of its insertions`. One private function writes it, and every read that cm6 leaves unguarded goes through that function. Each public function `panics --reach` gives it states the panic. The replayer needs no new rule: §10 "Engine errors" compares a captured `TypeError` by its name and leaves its message out, provided Ferrum's panic is a §5 message. The inventory of implicit throws missed these sites, because it took them for unreachable on the premise that step 4 refuted. F2 writes their rows in `raw/throws.jsonl` from harness runs: a second condition of `:305`, and new rows for `:416` and `:563`. (d) registers `set.invertMapCompose`, and (e) I1 writes the panic.

*Why not the alternatives.*
- *Leave Rust's `index out of bounds`.* D13 forbids a message that names nothing the caller can fix. §10 is right to fail it: a panic with a message outside §5 must stay a mismatch, or any unrelated bug would pass as an engine error.
- *Accept any panic where cm6 recorded a `TypeError`.* This would hide that same bug everywhere.
- *Keep the empty text in Ferrum's `map`, so the set stays whole.* `to_json` would then give a value where cm6 throws. That is a difference that no class of D11 allows.
- *Make `invert` check its document now.* That is an improvement, and its timing is D16's.

*The improvement it shows.* A caller that passes `invert` the wrong document should learn that at the call. As things stand, it learns it at a later, unrelated call, or never: a longer document gives an undo of the wrong text with no failure anywhere (F0354). History and collaboration invert every change. `state.imp.019` is a candidate for the delta. Its reference is a patch that throws in `invert` as `apply` does for the same mistake (`:208`). If it is adopted, the panic above can no longer fire through the public API, and it is removed in the same delta.

*Confidence.* High. *Reopens.* A harness run showing that one of the three reads gives a value rather than failing. That case would be D11 (c), reading past the end of an array, and would need its own row.

**Q153. How deep the fixtures pin a result's sharing.** *What the question assumed.* It assumed that whether F1's fixtures kill the mutant `conformance-text.jsonl:23` is measured only at (f). It is not. A mutation run of the four functions F1's generator targets, against the replay as it stands, takes minutes and answers it now. If the answer waited for (f), a missing check would surface after (d) and (e), and a second round would be needed for it alone.

*Is sharing below the top level worth pinning?* Yes. Ferrum keeps cm6's tree, because several results depend on its layout. Sharing nodes is how an edit avoids copying the document, which serves the goal of large documents without whole-document work. A mutant that copies deep nodes gives equal text and an equal shape, but costs time and memory that no value check shows. The replay already reads node identity through `__test` (`raw/replay.jsonl`, `text.sharedChildren`, `how: test`). A deeper count is the same kind of check, with values recorded from cm6.

*Decision.* Before F4's card, the orchestrator runs the mutants of `conformance-text.jsonl:5` to `:26`. If any survives, an op of chunk `text` that counts the nodes a result shares with its source through the whole tree goes into F4's card, and `round-c.ts` records it (about 15,000 more). If none survives, the op is not written: shape and the panic already pin what the finding is about.

*Lost.* Adding the op unconditionally: about 15,000, plus a replayer op, possibly for nothing. Waiting for (f).

*Confidence.* High.

**The cut of the rest of (c).** F1's transcript gives the figures (M39). The fixed part of a run is about 40,000. Reading a chunk's interpreter costs about 8,000. Each input line costs about 700 to read, and each group of cases written as one fixture set about 1,500. A new op costs about 10,000, writing and running a generator about 8,000, the rows about 4,000.
- *As one run,* chunks `rangeset`, `facet` and `state` come to about 230,000.
- *Split in two by chunk,* one part stays at about 155,000. Both figures are above the 140,000 at which `docs/workflow/README.md` §5 splits a card.
- *One run per chunk* gives about 115,000, 95,000 and 110,000, at the cost of two more fixed parts.

The runs go one after another, because all three write the same files and `spec_check` re-runs every generator.

Twelve lines are routed by their own evidence and not given to any of the three runs to read. For each, the evidence says either that cm6 has no counterpart value (a depth of the engine's stack, D17, the decided panic of `state.cm6.100.d2`, `from_json`'s `Err`) or that another step owns it (the replayer's rules, a mutant no test can give an input to). Reading them would cost about 8,000 and change nothing a fixture can hold.

The lines whose case has a value in cm6 stay with the fixtures, because a value from a run is the better evidence. These are the reads of a clone kept from a build that panicked (`conformance-facet.jsonl:191`, `:192`, `conformance-state.jsonl:63`'s first case, `:332`) and the nested `tr.state()` (`:427`, `:428`, `:401`). The `shared` lines are the threading of `design.md` §6, which (d) and (e) take. `conformance-change.jsonl:249` goes to (d), because no fixture can carry a float. Below 2^53 Ferrum must read an integral float as cm6 reads the same text, and at 2^53 it returns the domain's `Err`.

*Lost.*
- Two runs: one of them would sit at the cap with no margin, after a pilot that ended at 1.58 of its estimate.
- Giving the pre-routed lines to the runs to confirm: about 8,000 to read evidence that already names its step.

*Confidence.* Medium on the figures, since they come from one run of the kind; F2 measures them again (E74). High on the cut itself.

The replay therefore says nothing about whether Ferrum gives the row's value. These rows have a patch of cm6, though, and a patch writes Ferrum's observable result. The second difference is one nobody had named: in `state:F8400`, Ferrum calls field `a`'s `update` before it fails, while the patched cm6 fails before any callback runs. (d) W1 makes the replayer read a `decisions.jsonl` that it finds in the directory `FERRUM_REPLAY_FIXTURES` names.

## 2026-11-27 · Q155 and Q156: F4a's nine mismatches, and the op and seed of F4b

*Decided by the consultant (Q155 and Q156 of `ferrum-state`; the part for every crate: `docs/decisions/foundation.md`, "D1: a position beyond the document", and `docs/decisions/method.md`, "A generator G5 cannot reseed", same date). Binding text: `raw/decisions.jsonl` rows `state.cm6.126.d1` and `.126.d2`; `fixtures/no-counterpart.jsonl`; `raw/replay.jsonl` row `text.sharedNodes`; `raw/improvements.jsonl` `state.imp.004` (note) and `state.imp.020`; `plan.md` "Conformance" step 1 G5, step 5 (d), (e) and (f); `.claude/rules/decisions.md` D1; `docs/workflow/specification.md` §5 item 5. Ledger: E77.*

**Q155. Six fixtures are cases of rows already decided.** I wrote the lists and ran the checks.
- `state.cm6.126.d1` lists `state:F8434` and `F8435`: a `map` that returns `-0` for `0`, and `0` for `-0`.
- `state.cm6.126.d2` lists `state:F8429`, `F8431`, `F8432` and `F8433`: an effect whose value is `undefined`, through `StateEffect.map`, `mapEffects`, an update of two specs and `changeByRange`.
- `oracle_patch.py check` on both rows: each patch changes exactly its list. The control F8430 (`null`) is changed by neither.
- The replay then gives the six as `none` with the row's id. They were new left-out entries, so I wrote them with `FERRUM_REPLAY_WRITE_NO_COUNTERPART=1`. The diff of `fixtures/no-counterpart.jsonl` is those six lines and nothing else.
- G4's comparison with the patched output (`oracle_patch.py against`, (f)) covers them. By each row's rule I expect all six `equal`. I did not run the comparison, so (f) traces any difference before the tool is relied on.

**Q155. Three fixtures are defects for (e).** None of the three falls in a class of D11, so the floor is cm6's result and the fixture's recorded value is the expected one.
- *`state:F8421`, a state kept from a build that panicked.* `design.md` §6 already decides it: "After a build that panicked, reads on its own thread go on computing on demand, as in cm6". The fixture is the first case that shows Ferrum failing that sentence. `state.cm6.100.d2` covers a *transaction* from such a state, not a *read* of it, so its patch rightly leaves F8421 unchanged.
  - Ferrum gives an `unreachable!` for the slot `c` that was not yet computed. It gives `Cyclic dependency` for the second read of `c`, because the first read's panic left the slot computing.
  - cm6 gives 15, 15 and `[15]`, and `Cyclic dependency` only for `q`, whose own function threw.
  - The fix is (e) I2's, with `end_construction` (`conformance-state.jsonl:430`) and `:332`.
- *`state:F8438`, the nested `tr.state()`.* cm6 gives the outer build's state, because each constructor writes `tr._state` (`state.ts:58`). Ferrum gives the nested one, because its cell is written once.
  - *Is this class (b)?* No. The nested state handed to the callback is not changed. Only which state the transaction reports changes, and it changes before the build finishes, on the thread building it.
  - *Is it reproducible soundly?* Yes. `Transaction::state` returns a reference into the cell (`src/transaction.rs:402`), so the nested state must stay where it is. The cells are already a chain that only grows (`StateCell::next`), so the outer state can take another cell and become the one `find` returns, and the nested reference stays valid.
  - The case is now a fixture, so (d) no longer writes a test of it. (e) I2 fixes it with the state cell of `:401`.
- *`state:F8449`, a range whose `from` lies beyond the document.* cm6's `checkSelection` compares only `to` (`mirrors/cm6/state/src/selection.ts:241`). For a range with `from > to`, which cm6 makes by mapping (`state.cm6.087.d1`), `to` is the smaller end, so `create` keeps anchor 4 on a document of length 3.
  - Ferrum refuses it with D1's message, from `check_boundaries` (`src/state.rs:589`), which checks every anchor and head.
  - *What D1's rule is for.* It exists because a byte can fall inside a character. A position past the end falls inside nothing. The crate's own `Text` functions already treat it that way: they clip as cm6 clips and then check the boundary (`Text::replace`, `slice`, `slice_string`). `check_boundaries` is the one place that applies D1 to a position beyond the end, and it gives D1's message for a condition that is not D1's.
  - *What changes.* D1 is amended for every crate to say this ("D1: a position beyond the document" in `docs/decisions/foundation.md`). (e) I2 makes `check_boundaries` check only positions within the document, at `create`, at a transaction's selection and in `from_json`.
  - *Why not keep Ferrum's refusal as a deviation.* Refusing is arguably better, but no class of D11 allows it, and an improvement needs a reference that runs and its own moment (D16). I recorded it as a case of the candidate `state.imp.004`, which already gathers typed input cm6 accepts unchecked. Its reference is a patch comparing both ends, which gives cm6's own message, `Selection points outside of document`, at the call that took the range.

**Q155. An opportunity found on the way.** F8421 shows cm6 reporting a cycle for a slot whose function failed, and Ferrum reproduces that message. It is a misleading diagnosis for a developer, so it is the candidate `state.imp.020`, weighed at the improvement delta. Its value is low: only a caller that catches a failed build and goes on reading meets it.

**The replay test until (e).** It fails on `state:F8421`, `F8438` and `F8449`, and on nothing else. These three are the failing checks that (e) I2 makes pass (`.claude/rules/rust-crates.md` Tests 5), so I add no expected-failure list. That would be the skip list that the precedent forbids, and the test's failure output already names the three. Each (d) run confirms after its change that the failing set is exactly these three, and the plan's (d) says so. The mutation runs that count are (f)'s, after (e), so no baseline waits on a green replay before then. A probe of one mutant before (e) reads its own fixtures, not the whole replay.

**Q156. The op's row.** `raw/replay.jsonl` now has `text.sharedNodes`, `how` `test`, as its pair `text.sharedChildren` has. The count is a walk over the public `children` (D2), with each node compared by `__test::same_text`, the identity that `design.md` §10 gives `Text`. The walk follows the interpreter's own comment (`oracle/cm6/text/interp.ts:115-120`).
- (d) W1 registers it. The 45 `pending` fixtures should then compare `equal` or `part` at the current code. The ops count shared nodes, and the unmutated code is not the defect: `:24` and `:25` are surviving mutants, which the op now kills at (f).

**Q156. The seed.** I accept F4b's formula as it stands. G5 needs three things from a seed rule:
- The default is unchanged, so the committed fixtures regenerate byte for byte. They do: `spec_check` regenerates them.
- No salt repeats another salt's draws. Multiplying by an odd constant modulo 2^64, then XOR with 7, is one-to-one over the salts G5 passes.
- No salt of 1 or more gives the default. That would take a salt whose product is 0, which an odd multiplier never gives for a nonzero salt below 2^64.

Which mixing function is used is not observable to anything that consumes the fixtures. What failed was silent: the chains ignored the salt from F1 until F4b, and G5 would have counted the chunk's other generators and never noticed. So the rule is now written where generators are written (`docs/workflow/specification.md` §5 item 5, and the plan's G5), and a check that the orchestrator adds to `tools/spec_check.py` before G5 makes the class show (plan step 5 (f), E77). The method's part is in `docs/decisions/method.md`.
- *Size.* A salt adds about 0.45 MB of chains and their runtime. That is a cost in disk and seconds, and none in tokens. G5 stops drawing for a chunk once it has its 5,000 cases. I put no cap on the chains: the cases of `:24` and `:25` need the large documents, and those are exactly what the fresh seeds should draw.

**Lost.**
- *F8421 or F8438 as deviations.* No class allows either, and §6 already says the opposite for F8421.
- *Keeping D1's refusal of F8449 now.* It is a difference with no reference that runs, made in the middle of conformance.
